All 23 scenarios end with the enemy spawn destroyed, errors 0, and **every previously existing scenario finishes on exactly the same tick as before** — on the stub the tower is bought in one scenario only (`tower+hover`, t≈870) and changes nothing there, because the stub's opponents are weaker than the live ones: none of them takes our house. That is the honest state of the measurement — the machinery is proven, its value is not, and the instrument that can prove it is a live series, not the stub.

Not fixed, and named — **and then measured down the same day, see the road probe below: there is no traffic to pave.** What follows was the reasoning at the time and it turned out to rest on a wrong premise: **the economy is bound by loaded movement on swamp, and roads are the answer we have not built.** A round trip to a typical point is about thirty steps out and thirty back, and the loaded leg costs five ticks a cell — 180 ticks for 250 energy, which is why twelve haulers deliver eleven a tick against a target of twenty-seven. A road makes that leg one tick a cell: 60 ticks for the same load, three times the throughput, for fifty energy per swamp cell laid once. Doubling `MOVE` per `CARRY` is exactly energy-neutral by the same arithmetic (both come to 360 energy per unit of income) and buys only a smaller fleet for the same income; roads are the change that moves the number. What stops it today is that the containers worth hauling appear at random and decay in about a hundred ticks, so a road network has to be planned against the *distribution* of points rather than against today's points — which is the same demand the operator's first frame makes of every number here.

**Twenty-fifth version** (match 26, 05.09.2026, lost at t=800 to stachu3478 — the first match played by v24). Two things came back from it. The **constants probe** printed what the arena itself reports, which is what the next structural step has to be built on: `rampartHits=10000 wallHits=10000 extension=100/100 maxSites=10 obstacles=["creep","tower","constructedWall","spawn","extension","link"]` — a rampart is two hundred energy for ten thousand hits, the same as a wall for a hundred, and neither can ever be topped up because the arena `Creep` has no `repair`; the obstacle list is the engine's, and it names no rampart, so a hostile rampart blocks by the engine's separate rule rather than by type, exactly as the movement code here already assumes. And the **tower's efficiency term was measured with the wrong instrument**: `homeShare` counted ticks under *alarm*, which means an armed enemy within forty ticks of approach, and in this match the fight was at the corner container at `(3,14)` — thirty-five cells from our spawn at `(5,50)` — while `homeShare` read 47 % and then 81 %. A tower reaches twenty cells and falls fifty damage a cell; the honest measure of how much of the time it would be firing is its own range, so the window now counts ticks with a combat enemy inside `TOWER_FALLOFF_RANGE` of the spawn. The second correction is the gate: the site and the keeper stood behind `!fighterFirst`, and `fighterFirst` is true precisely when the tower is wanted — an enemy at the gates. The trace shows the cost exactly: `worth=true` three times in that match, a site placed zero times. But `towerWorth` has *already* asked whether a fighter is the better buy, against these enemies, with the measured death rate of our fighters in it; asking the same question a second time in a cruder form can only overrule the answer that looked at the numbers. What remains is the clock, which is a different question: we save for the keeper only while `spawnLife` outlasts the saving, the same condition the camp rule uses, and the keeper draws from the spawn unless the house is critical (under fire with the garrison losing). Stub: 23/23, `tower+hover` 943→993 — the fifty ticks are the tower being bought earlier in the one scenario that buys it, and that scenario did not need it.

What the same log says about the fight itself, and what it is **not** fixed by: at t=650 six of our fighters stood at 1794 by our own power measure against 1272, and forty ticks later two were dead and the enemy had lost about five per cent. His group was `M4R5`, `M2R5` and **two** `T1M5H3`; seventy-two heal a tick against our nominal two hundred and forty damage, of which perhaps a hundred and sixty was in range — the rest were walking. Our body at that budget is `M4R4M4`: forty damage and 1200 hits for a thousand, against his `M4R5`'s fifty and 1000 for nine hundred and fifty. He buys guns and cannot move (four MOVE under five heavy parts is two ticks a cell on plain, seven on swamp); we buy the mobility to reach him and then lose the standing fight at his chosen ground. Neither the tower nor anything else in this version touches that.

**Twenty-sixth version — in the home fight, only the fighters that make it count (05.09.2026).** Matches 24, 25 and 26 all end the same way and the tick lines say the same thing each time: our own measure calls the home fight ours and we lose it. Match 26 at t=610 reads `home=fight:wins(1301/616)` — a claim that we are twice his strength — and forty ticks later two of our six are dead and his pack has lost about five per cent of its power. The arithmetic of the claim is sound and its inputs are not: `homeOurs` summed **every** armed fighter not in a wave, wherever it stood, while the fight was decided by the three that were inside range at the time. Six creeps around a four-creep cluster do not all fire; the ones still walking contribute nothing, and Lanchester's square law assumes the opposite. So `homeOurs`/`homeTheirs` are now computed over `homeReady` — the fighters whose own path to the nearest home threat (`pathTicks` down a flow field, each creep by its own body over swamp) fits inside the time the line survives, `Σ our hits / their damage`. `homeMode` prints the count: `fight:wins(1301/616[4/6])`.

The window matters and the first attempt used the wrong one. Time-to-the-first-casualty (`average hits / their dps`, about eight ticks against a melee ball) is the wrong question — the fight lasts until one line is gone, and a reinforcement arriving halfway does fight in it; with the short window the guard stopped going out to meet anything and the stub lost real tempo (`ball` 664→897, `tower+healball` 965→1039). With the line's own lifetime as the window, every scenario returns to its previous tick except `raider` (502→509), and the change is free where it does not apply. This is the geometry the healer investigation named as missing, in the place where it costs the most: not "who can be healed" but **who is in the fight at all**.

Second correction in the same version, from the same series: `towerWorth` bought a tower on a **zero tie**. With our fighters dead and his heal above a single fighter's damage, both marginal gains are exactly zero and `0 >= 0` placed a site (match 31, `share=9% surv=17% base=0 tower=0 fighter=0`). A purchase now needs a strictly positive gain — when nothing adds power, nothing is worth buying.

Live, and the result names its own cause: the v25 series (tower reach + gate, no `homeReady`) went 4-1-1 with two wins over stachu3478; the v26 series went **7-1-1, rating 1102→1120** — four wins over けろびー and three over stachu3478, the two players who had been beating us all season. **No tower was built in a single match of either series**, so the gain is the power-model correction and nothing else: the bot stopped walking into fights its own arithmetic had already lost. The tower machinery is built, proven mechanically on the stub and idle in every live match so far — its criterion is strict by construction, and the only two matches where it fired were already lost. That is where it stands: a capability we did not have, whose value is still unmeasured.

**The exchange veto — built, fixed twice, measured three times and withdrawn (versions 27-30, 06.09.2026).** The diagnosis behind it is solid and stands: in match 33 the bot read `home=fight:wins(1081/616[5/5])` — every one of five fighters could reach the fight in time — and lost three of them in sixty ticks while his four (two `M5R5` and two `T1M5H3`, seventy-two heal) lost nothing. `[5/5]` proves arrival was not the constraint: only two of ours stood inside range at once, and eighty damage against seventy-two heal is eight a tick into 3800 hits. The power model sums the whole group's damage and subtracts his heal once, so in that shape it is wrong by a factor of ten, and it kept declaring a win while we fed him one creep at a time.

The remedy followed the pattern that already works here — the model predicts, the measurement vetoes, exactly as `realisedIncome` vetoes `projectedIncome`. `measureExchange` counts each side's **net** hit loss (net, because a hit healed back is not progress, and that is precisely what fools the count), a dead creep counting its last hits in full; the model's prediction is his damage over our net damage, i.e. how many of our hits should buy one of his; and once the evidence amounts to a whole fighter's worth, a trade worse than predicted by `DEFEND_MARGIN` drops the fight to a held post.

It needed two repairs before it did anything at all. **v28:** the counter reset whenever the home fight went off for a single tick, and an enemy steps in and out of the forty-tick alarm ring every few ticks — the log reads `x100/56 → x0/0 → (none) → x20/144` with two of five fighters lost between those lines; the evidence was erased faster than it accrued, so the veto never once fired. It became a rolling window, counted every tick over every armed creep on both sides (filtering by "near home" would drop a creep that stepped out of the ring from the live list and score it as a kill). **v29:** the window then remembered too much. By t=650 of match 35 we had eight whole fighters against three battered ones — 320 damage against 100 with 36 heal, a fight taken in eight ticks — and the veto still cited a trade from three hundred ticks earlier, when his army was twice the size. The window became the life of our own line, our hits over his current damage, bounded by the approach and production windows so that it is a computed scale rather than a chosen number.

**And then the measurement said no.** v26, without any of this: 7-1-1 over nine matches, four wins over けろびー. v27, v28 and v29: one match each, all three lost to the same けろびー, rating 1120→1087. The stub had been saying it in its own currency all along — `tower+hover` 993→1196, `harass` 901→963, `stream17` 951→1023. The form was right and the substance was not: **the veto also calls off the fights we would win, and leaves him the field — and the field here is the energy that decides the match.** Every losing log ends the same way, with our fighters whole and idle at the post and income at five against a target of twenty-seven.

Withdrawn in v30; every scenario returned to its v26 tick, and the next series went 2-1. The numbers stay in the log — `x<ours>/<his>~<predicted>w<window>` — because the discrepancy is real, tenfold in the lost matches, and it wants a correction **inside** the power model rather than a patch bolted on top of it. The shape of that correction is now named by two independent measurements: the model credits every creep in the group with firing from the first tick, while in a real engagement only those inside `RANGED_RANGE` fire, and against a healed opponent the difference between three guns and five is the difference between progress and none at all. Recorded as a limitation only after the removal was built, repaired twice and measured three times.

**Thirty-first version — a fighter that neither reaches nor catches is not a gun (06.09.2026, match 36 lost to stachu3478).** This is the correction the previous four versions were groping for, and the log finally shows the mechanism rather than the symptom. At t=610 the count read `home=fight:wins(1081/616[5/5])`: five fighters, every one of them able to arrive in time. Over the next forty ticks his pair — two `M5R5` and two `T1M5H3` — walked from `(88,21)` to `(97,20)` **shooting as it went**. We lost 3929 hits and it lost 1414; and by t=700 it was whole again, because heal restores body parts as well as hits: the `M1R5` at 564/1000 at t=650 is an `M5R5` at 1000/1000 at t=700, and two `M5H3` return 4320 hits a minute.

That is the asymmetry no static count can see. **Damage on us is permanent and damage on him is a loan**, so a fight we do not *finish* is a pure loss — and this one could not be finished, because his `M5R5` covers a plain cell per tick exactly as our `M4R4M4` does, and a retreating pair at equal speed is never caught. The bot already had the right predicate: `catchable` asks whether a target can be closed with, and answers from **measurement** — did it step further away last tick (`enemyPrevCell`) — rather than from a speed table. It was only ever asked about a melee creep defending at home. Now the home count asks it of every fighter: one that is outside `RANGED_RANGE` and cannot close is not counted as damage, whatever the arithmetic says. Nothing is ceded by this — they must come to the spawn to win, and a creep standing next to our spawn is not retreating, so the same predicate turns the fight back on by itself.

Stub 23/23 and, unlike the exchange veto, it moves the right way: `harass` 901→**779** — and `harass` is precisely the kiting scenario, two `M5R1` that back off from a fighter — `rush` 708→690, `tower+healball` 965→978, everything else on its old tick. Live: **four wins and then a loss**, three of the wins over stachu3478 and one over けろびー, rating 1077→1089; the version before it had lost its first match. In the fifth match the economy was finally healthy — nine haulers, realised income up to fourteen against a projection of eight — and the army still evaporated from five to one in the last forty ticks with `homeAll` empty, which is the next thread: our fighters end matches as husks whose weapons are shot off while their hits remain.

**Thirty-second version — the guns are the last part of the body to die, not the first (06.09.2026).** Match 40 ended with three husks standing at a burning spawn — `f33` at 5/1700, `f36` at 165/1200, `f30` at 55/1200 — every one of them with its legs intact and its weapons shot off. The body was built `[MOVE×4, RANGED×4, MOVE×4]`, damage takes parts from the front, so after 800 taken a fighter has four legs and no guns; it walks home and does nothing. His `M5R5` is `[MOVE×5, RANGED×5]`, and the log prints it as `M1R5 564/1000` — one live leg and **all five guns**. He loses his walk and keeps shooting; we lose the shooting and keep walking.

The first attempt got the diagnosis right and the remedy wrong. To let a gun-last body score properly it removed the `moves < weight` break from `bodyValue` ("speed lost — the rest of the body is no use to the wave"), and **TOUGH ate the valuation within one run**: the chosen body became `ttttt mmmmmmmmmm rrr` — three guns in eighteen parts — and `harass` stopped taking the enemy spawn at all. TOUGH is the cheapest hits in the game and that break is the only thing holding it down; it stays.

The second attempt separates two questions that had been answered by one function. **How much of each part to buy** is scored by `bodyValue` on the old arrangement, exactly as before, and TOUGH stays dominated. **Which part to lose first** is a different question with its own reason — damage comes from the front — and the body is emitted with every `MOVE` ahead of the guns. The composition is unchanged (the same `M8R4` by count); only the order is. The fighter now stays at full speed until 400 taken, drops to half at 600, and keeps all four guns until 800, ending as a turret that still shoots rather than a runner that cannot.

Stub 23/23 and it moves broadly the right way: `tower+hover` 993→**853**, `towersite` 839→**772**, `tower+pairs` 843→**777**, `tower` 838→778, `tower+enemy` 877→838, `tower+stream` 1015→964, `freeze` 575→543, `enemy` 464→434; against it `fortress` 1381→1455, `stream17` 1004→1058, `pairs` 446→484. Live it settled the question: the first match after it was lost, which is what one match is worth — a fixed eight-match series with no early stop then went **seven wins and a draw, no losses**, rating 1080→**1125**, four wins over stachu3478 and three plus the draw over けろびー. Both of the season's leaders were beaten in the same sitting.

**Versions 33 and 34 — the approach to the enemy spawn is priced in damage taken, and the clock walks the same route (06.09.2026).** The long draws were all one thing, and the posture line says it in a sentence: `push=false(defend)` for the whole match, his spawn still `3000/3000`, and `towers=1 sim=lose/62t/-5191` while the wave held 5597 hits. **The entire loss is the tower's fire over sixty-two ticks.** A tower reaches twenty cells and deals a hundred a tick; our wave crosses that circle over swamp at three ticks a cell and so takes six times the shots a plain crossing would. The field the wave walked was built on shortest time and knew nothing about the fire. And leaving earlier was not available: his site is visible from t=200 with an ETA the bot computes correctly (205→155→105→55, tower fed at ~t=405), our travel is 157 ticks, so departure would have had to be at t≈248 — when we had one fighter.

So `DistanceMap.flowFieldTo` gained an optional per-cell price — the damage taken there — and the field now minimises damage rather than length: round the circle where there is a way round, across it over plain rather than swamp where there is not. The siege simulation walks the same field, so its verdict describes the route the wave will really take. With no fed tower the field is the old one cell for cell. Stub: `fortress`, the three-tower scenario, **1455→1167**; everything else within noise, which is what a single tower standing on the spawn should give — there is no way round it worth taking.

**And it lost, until the clocks were fixed.** v33 kept `startTravel`, `frontTravel` and `homeTravel` on the plain field while the wave walked a longer one, so the departure budget, the sortie window and "will we be back in time" were all optimistic by exactly the length of the detour. The stub could not see it — its detours are short enough that route ticks and plain ticks agree — and the live measurement charged for it precisely: **v32 7-1-0, v33 4-2-2 on the same sample of eight.** v34 puts the clocks on the assault route with `pathTicks`, which counts each creep's own body over the cells it will actually cross, and the same eight-match series returns **6-1-1, rating 1119→1143 — and rank 1 for the first time this season at match five.**

The lesson worth keeping is not about towers. **A route and the clock that budgets it must be the same route**, and when they are not the error is invisible to the harness and expensive in the match: the stub reported v33 and v34 as identical in all 23 scenarios, and live they are two wins apart.

**Thirty-fifth version — hauler capacity is bought against the supply flow, not the pile on the ground (06.09.2026).** Match 45 held income at 7-11 against a target of 27 for all nine hundred ticks with ten to thirteen thousand lying on the ground, and the fleet stuck at eight haulers. The reason computes exactly: `capacityBound` asks whether the ground holds four rounds for the whole fleet — eight haulers by 250 by four is 8000 — and 6050 was lying there, so no ninth hauler was bought. But the site line says that number is not a stock: `C(58,17)e=2000d=4 C(58,95)e=2000d=4 C(26,31)e=2000d=54 C(26,89)e=2000d=54` — the map drops two containers of 2000 every fifty ticks, **eighty energy a tick appears** while we carry nine away. The rule read a flow as a warehouse and answered "not enough ground" where what was short was us.

`measureSupply` counts what appears — each site the first time it is seen, with the energy it appeared holding — over the same window as every other measurement here, and while more appears than we carry, capacity is the bottleneck by definition. It is an alternative to `capacityBound`, not a replacement: measured delivery still vetoes growth when the fleet does not carry what it projects, and the spawn's target still vetoes it when there is nothing left to convert. The tick line prints `s80`, and the live log confirms the arithmetic to the digit — the arena really does supply eighty a tick.

Live: the fleet reached **thirteen haulers** where it used to stop at seven or eight, income 7→12 with realised 9, and the series went 5-1-2, rating 1143→**1168**. Stub 23/23, at a cost of tempo where a scenario ends before the capacity pays for itself (`tower` 777→818, `fortress` 1167→1215, `tower+pairs` 776→810, against `tower+stream` 966→947).

**What it also measured is the next limit.** Doubling the fleet raised income by seventy per cent, not by a hundred: each extra hauler adds about 0.7 a tick because the near energy goes first and the trips lengthen. Reaching the spawn's target of 27 this way would need about thirty haulers against a cap of sixteen. The loaded leg is what costs — five ticks a swamp cell — and that is a road's business, not a hauler's.

**Thirty-sixth version — the economy-versus-army hedge counts living force, not money spent (06.09.2026).** v35 removed one brake and uncovered another defect behind it. The 1999-tick draw: at t=810 we were pushing with twelve haulers and four fighters and had his spawn down to 2200; by t=1210 the fleet was fifteen, the army three, his spawn back at 3000, and we sat out the draw with fifteen haulers carrying six a tick. The rule allowed it without complaint, because `spentFighters` accumulates every fighter ever built, the dead included: having sunk eight thousand into fighters and holding two alive, we were entitled to nine thousand of fleet. A dead creep went on justifying a hauler for the rest of the match.

The hedge now compares what exists — the cost of the live parts of the live fleet against the cost of the live parts of the live armed. A part that has been shot off is worth nothing as damage and nothing as hits, and counting it in a comparison of forces is counting a ghost. `survivalOfFighters` already needed exactly that sum, so it is one helper (`liveCost`) now, and the hauler line prints `live=<fleet>/<army>`.

Measured on the same kind of match, it does exactly what it was built to do — the army stops collapsing and the delivery follows it up:

| tick | before (v35) | after (v36) |
|---|---|---|
| 810 | 12 haulers / 4 fighters | 11 / **6** |
| 1210 | 15 / **3**, realised **2** | 16 / **6**, realised **16** |
| 1610 | 15 / **2**, realised 6 | 16 / **10**, realised **18** |

The first eight-match series read 4-2-2 against v35's 5-1-2, rating 1168→1162 — but seven of those eight were against stachu3478 where v35's had been mostly けろびー, so that number compared two different opponents while the in-match numbers compared one. A second series settled it: **6-2-0, rating 1162→1177**, the session's high, and over sixteen matches v36 stands at 10-4-2. The first series was the draw of opponents, as the in-match numbers had already said. Stub 23/23, every scenario on the same tick: there the army does not die away, so the hedge never binds, which is the point of it.

**Thirty-seventh version — an enemy sitting on our energy is a threat to the house (06.09.2026).** The 1999-tick draw at t=1210: fourteen haulers, **seven fighters standing at home**, eighty energy a tick appearing on the map — and two thousand of it usable, income two. A site with a combat enemy within `SITE_DANGER_RANGE` is struck out of the usable set entirely, so the fleet carried air while the army watched. That is not "there is no energy"; it is a field we have conceded, and conceding it costs the whole economy.

A creep holding one of our sites now counts as a home threat, and every gate already in place decides the rest: `homeWins` does the arithmetic, `homeReady` drops whoever cannot get there in time, `guardHolds` keeps the house covered. Only sites on our side of the map and worth a full hauler's trip qualify — nobody walks across the map for scraps. And it is not the chase v31 forbade: **a creep parked on a container is not retreating**, so `catchable` is true of it by measurement rather than assumption, and when it does leave the same predicate lets it go.

In-match it does what it was built for. On the same kind of draw the realised income stops collapsing and the army holds:

| tick | before (v36) | after (v37) |
|---|---|---|
| 810 | 11 haulers / 5 fighters, realised 10 | 11 / 4, realised **14** |
| 1210 | 14 / 7, realised **6** | 15 / 6, realised **12** |
| 1610 | 13 / **2**, realised **3** | 16 / **10**, realised **11** |

Over sixteen matches it went 10-2-4 (wins-draws-losses reads 10 wins, 2 draws, 4 losses), rating 1177→**1197** with a peak of 1208 — the season's high. Against v36's 10-4-2 that is the same ten wins with draws traded for losses, which is what a posture that goes out to contest the field should look like: more matches decided, in both directions. Stub 23/23 with only `tower+healball` moving, by six ticks — its opponents go for the spawn, not for the sites, so the harness barely exercises this and the live series had to carry the judgement alone.

**Thirty-eighth version — an unfinished tower is the worst outcome of all (06.09.2026).** The home-share rule written in v25 finally started firing in live matches — `share` 31 to 64 per cent, a site placed in four of them, a keeper built — and **not one tower was ever finished**. The site reached `800/1250` and stood there to the end of the match: eight hundred into the ground and seven hundred into the keeper, fifteen hundred energy for nothing at all, which is worse than either building it or not starting.

Two causes, both in gates rather than in the tower. The keeper drew from the spawn only while the house was not "critical", and critical meant alarm with our power below his — under a camp that is the permanent state, so the keeper stopped and never resumed. The clock here is the one the camp rule already uses and states honestly: you may not save while the spawn will not outlive the saving. So the keeper draws whenever the spawn outlives a full fighter's build and stops when it does not. And the decision was taken on the **full** price throughout: with eight hundred already spent the tower costs four hundred and fifty, and that is what has to be compared against a fighter. What is spent is spent and decides nothing; `towerWorth` now takes the remaining cost, and while a site stands the question asked each tick is whether to finish it, not whether to have started.

Live: **7-1-0, rating 1197→1210**, the season's high and no draws at all — and `mine=T(92,47)h=3000e=10` in the log, **the first tower we have ever completed**, standing at full hits with a shot loaded. It never had to fire in that match; the point is that fifteen hundred energy stopped being burned. Stub 23/23 on the same ticks, since the criterion never fires there.

**Roads — probed and rejected, 06.09.2026 (versions 39 and 40).** With supply, fleet size and the contested-site rule all fixed, income had run into one number: twelve haulers delivering 1.17 a tick each, a round trip of 214 ticks. A road makes a loaded swamp step one tick instead of five for fifty energy, so the arithmetic looked inviting — but it turned on an unknown the constants do not settle. `ROAD_HITS` and `ROAD_WEAROUT` say 500 and 1, and how much a step actually takes off does not follow from them: a road lasts forty crossings or five hundred, and the value moves by an order of magnitude between those. The client typings do not say, and the stub does not model wear at all.

So it was measured rather than argued. The keeper now outlives the tower it builds, so v39 had it lay **one** road on the swamp cell our loaded haulers had spent the most ticks on, and print that road's hits every fifty ticks. Nothing else changed — not the paths, not the movement rules, not the hauler's choice.

The answer came back negative and complete. **The busiest swamp cell on the whole map had six loaded-hauler ticks in a 1999-tick match.** A road there saves four ticks a crossing: six crossings, twenty-four ticks, for fifty energy. Wear is not the reason — there was none at all, `hits=2500/2500` for eleven hundred ticks — and it also corrected a constant: **`ROAD_HITS` is 2500 in the runtime, not the 500 the client typings claim**, the same staleness already found in the tower's falloff range. The reason is that **the traffic does not concentrate**: the flow field already routes round swamp at five times the cost, and the containers appear at random points across a hundred-by-hundred map, so no shared corridor forms that would be worth paving.

That also corrects the estimate that motivated the probe. The 214-tick round trip is not mostly loaded swamp walking; the trip is long because the map is large and the drops are far. Withdrawn in v40, with the finding written above `runBuilders` so nobody re-derives it, and the stub constant corrected. The eight matches the probe rode along with went 3-3-2 (rating 1210→1201) — one road was laid in one of them, so that series says nothing about the probe and everything about a run of けろびー draws.

**A second spawn — rejected on its premise, 06.09.2026, before building it.** The forward spawn けろびー builds three of per match was the last candidate on the economy: it does not speed the loaded leg, it moves the destination, so it answers both the income and the two-hundred-tick march at once, for the price of one fighter. Checking the premise first killed it. **Our spawn is busy only 34 to 52 per cent of the ticks, and while idle it holds 332 to 380 energy on average** — it is idle because it is saving, not because it is out of work. A second spawn adds production capacity we have no energy to use: realised income is 14 where a single spawn converts 27.

Following that number closes the whole economic thread with a chain rather than a guess. Income is bounded by fleet capacity; fleet capacity is bounded by the living-force hedge, which will not let the fleet exceed the army by more than one spawn's worth; the army stands at two to four thousand of live energy because **it keeps dying**. So income is bounded by the army's survival — not by the spawn, not by roads (measured and rejected above), not by the number of haulers. Every remaining economic lever is downstream of the fighters staying alive, and that is the heal asymmetry measured since match 22: his damage is a loan and ours is permanent.

**Where the army's hits go — measured, and the answer is "nowhere avoidable" (v41, 06.09.2026).** With the economic thread closed on the army's survival, the natural suspicion was position: fighters standing where they are shot without shooting back. `measureIncoming` splits every hit we lose into three buckets by the situation of the tick it was dealt in — an enemy inside **our** range with our guns alive (a trade), an enemy inside our range with the guns already shot off (a hulk), and no enemy inside our range at all (unanswerable). The bucketing needs no change to the firing code, because `shoot()` fires at whatever is in reach: "an enemy within three and guns alive" is the same statement as "we answered". It validated itself on the stub, where `tower+pairs` reads `trade=443 unanswerable=1400` and the fourteen hundred is the enemy tower, which reaches twenty cells where we reach three.

Live, over four matches: `trade=8420 husk=0 unanswerable=0`, `trade=16608 husk=0 unanswerable=0`, `trade=7343 husk=0 unanswerable=0`, `trade=8198 husk=0 unanswerable=0`. **Zero, every time.** Eight to sixteen thousand hits a match, all of them taken with an enemy in our own range and our own guns firing back. There is no positional loss to recover and no hulk problem to fix: the army dies in fair exchanges that we lose on the rate, and the rate is his heal — seventy-two a tick against our nothing.

That closes the last cheap line of attack and leaves exactly one: **the exchange rate itself.** Both previous approaches to it failed the same way — heal inside the body (the valuation picked one gun and two heals) and a healer creep chosen against a fighter by score (the bot built forty-five healers and one fighter) — because heal enters the power model as a subtraction from the enemy's damage and is therefore unbounded there. What has not been tried is the form the opponent actually fields: a **formation with a hard structural cap** — at most one healer per living armed fighter, chosen by no score at all, so the degeneracy is not merely discouraged but impossible. This session's own record says structural rules survive where valuations degenerate: `catchable` in v31 and the body order in v32 both worked; every scoring change in the same area was measured down.

**Forty-second version — the focus target is the one that dies soonest under the guns that reach it (06.09.2026).** With position ruled out by v41's buckets, the replay of a fresh loss put the old number back exactly where match 22 had left it, and showed that v23's local rule had never moved it: **his focus share 0.95 at 1.14 distinct targets a tick, ours 0.75 at 1.68.** Silence was not the cause — we fired in 104 of 110 opportunities and he in 181 of 182. His damage converges on one creep and ours spreads over one and a half, and against a healer returning thirty-six a tick that decides everything: damage split two ways is covered and damage concentrated is not.

The cause was in the ordering of the global focus target. It ranked heal first, so it picked a healer **one** gun could reach — forty damage against thirty-six heal, two hundred and twenty-five ticks to kill, which is spreading in the shape of focus. The obvious correction, putting "most guns bear" above heal, is also wrong and the stub said so immediately: `tower+healball` 976→1231. The right question is one, and it dissolves the lexicography altogether: **which target dies soonest under the guns that actually reach it, net of the heal that reaches it.** A healer under one gun scores infinity; a healer under four scores the minimum and is chosen; a shooter under four beats a healer under one, as it should. Stub 23/23 with `stream17` 1058→**888** and `tower+healball` back at 982 against a baseline of 976.

Live, and this is the cleanest confirmation in the bot's history: the series went **6-1-1, rating 1192→1220**, four of five matches against けろびー won — and the replay of one of those wins reads **our focus share 0.93 at 1.24 targets a tick against his 0.50 at 2.00**, with 206 shots to his 58. The instrument named the defect, the change moved exactly the quantity the instrument measures, and the rating followed.

**Forty-third version — the tower is killed, not endured, while it costs no more than the spawn (06.09.2026).** The draw that motivated it: at t=1500 his tower was up and five of ours stood ready, and the departure was refused because five against a tower simulates as a loss. Eight were reached at t=1750, the front at t=1900, the simulation then said `win/51t` — and the match ended ninety-nine ticks later. We finished six times stronger, 2089 against 328, and were late by about fifty ticks. The old rule — *"towers fire for the whole fight, we do not finish them, they are not the target"* — was true at a focus share of 0.75, where spread fire into a three-thousand-hit structure is pointless. At 0.93 eight guns take one down in ten ticks, and it is worth a hundred a tick until the match ends, against this wave and every one after it.

The rule is stated once and used in **both** places, which is what took four attempts. Soft targets first — creeps, then towers, then the spawn — since creeps both shoot and die faster while the spawn does not shoot at all; and towers only while **all** of those that reach us cost no more than the spawn. At equal hits, which is what this arena gives (3000 each), the tower is the better buy: the damage taken is the same and it never fires again. Three towers of a `fortress` are nine thousand against three, and chewing them instead of the target is a loss by definition — the rule skips them, and `fortress` is unchanged.

Each earlier attempt failed by disagreeing with itself, and the stub named each one immediately: the simulation chasing towers the shooting would not touch (`fortress` +130), and a value measure that scored a healer at zero because a healer deals no damage (`tower+healball` +374). Stub, finally: `tower` 818→**496**, `towersite` 772→**487**, `tower+hover` 867→**496**, `tower+enemy` 853→829, `fortress` unchanged, against `tower+healball` 982→1030 — a thousand ticks of gain against forty-eight of loss, 23/23.

Live: a draw, then **four wins in a row**, then a loss — rating 1213→**1227** at the peak, the season's high.

**Forty-fourth version — the tower gets a clock.** The first thing the new series instrument said
(`tools/series.py metrics`, whole matches, twenty-four of them) was that the two fields standing furthest
apart between wins and losses are `mine#3` and `mine#4` — the progress of **our own tower site**. Read per
match (`series.py field`), it is stark: a site was placed four times, it finished once — in a won match,
where the tower stood with 3000 hits — and the other three times it never finished, and all three of those
matches are losses (863/1250, 325/1250, 220/1250). The loss of 06.09 22:22 shows the mechanism: from tick
900, with the army down to three husks (`our=0/225`), seven of his creeps at home and income at 1-2 a tick,
the spawn twice logged "saving for builder", bought one for 700, and the site crawled 768 → 863 of 1250 to
the end of the match. Some fifteen hundred energy into nothing at the exact moment a body was the only
thing that mattered. The cause is not a threshold: `towerWorth` compares the **gain per energy** of a tower
against that of a fighter, and there is no time in that comparison at all. A fighter is born in tens of
ticks; a site becomes a tower only when the rest of its price is *delivered*, and at a dead economy that is
never. What makes it a plain defect rather than a hard question is that the bot already computes exactly
this clock — for the **enemy's** site (`pendingTowers`: observed progress rate, and a site that is not
moving is not counted as a tower). Its own site had no clock. So v44 gives it the same one, and gives it to
all three decisions that spend on a tower: placing the site, buying the keeper, and **feeding what is
already started** — `runBuilders` no longer carries energy into a site that cannot finish, and a keeper
holding energy with nowhere to put it returns it to the spawn. The horizon is made of quantities that
already exist: the spawn's life under the current incoming fire, and the ticks left in the match. Two
things were got wrong on the way and both were caught by the fixture: the observed rate must be trusted
only when it is **positive** (a zero means the keeper has not arrived yet, and the first attempt froze a
healthy site ten ticks after buying its keeper), and the delivery clock must use the **measured** income
(`realisedIncome`, averaged over the production window) rather than this tick's projection. Measurement:
all twenty-two outcome scenarios are tick-for-tick identical to v43 — this rule fires only where the
economy is dead, and none of the old scenarios has a dead economy. The new one does: `siege` puts standing
hunters at our gates who shoot haulers and never kite, calibrated against that lost match rather than
against a desired answer (five of them reproduce its shape: income 4-15, the fleet alive, the siege lasting
a thousand ticks). At six hunters the income goes to zero and the site becomes a dead one: v43 leaves 649
energy in it, v44 leaves 10. **The `siege` outcome is deliberately not a gate**: with the same build, moving
the reinforcement period by one tick gives 938, 1631 and 1685 — the fixture is chaotic in its outcome and
carries signal only in its decision, so `regress.sh` asserts the decision (`run2:siege6`, pass = at most a
keeper's carrying capacity of energy abandoned) and leaves the outcome out of the pass list.

**Forty-fifth version — the rampart is part of the price of the spawn.** The operator pointed at what the
readings kept missing: in some draws the wave was not failing to arrive, it was failing to get *through*
— a rampart over the enemy spawn, with a tower killing us while we chewed it. The logs settle it. In nine
of the ten matches of the v44 series the enemy had ramparts (both opponents build them), and in the draw
of 07.09 00:04 the enemy spawn's hits stood at 3000 from tick 10 to tick 1760 and then fell 3000 → 2080 →
1120 → 440 → 360 in thirty ticks: the rampart held every shot until it died, and the match ended 360 hits
short of a win. In the draw of 00:09 the same shape with a tower in it — his tower chewed down to 180 of
3000, his spawn at 2960, our fighters standing three cells from his spawn with 151, 400 and 745 hits of
1200. So the job at his spawn is not 3000 hits, it is 13000, and **three places in the bot price that job
while only one of them counted the rampart**: the siege simulation had it (`spawnHits = spawn.hits * ratio
+ rampartHits`), while both "tower before the spawn" rules — the one in the simulation and the one in the
live fire — compared the towers' hits against the bare 3000. With a fortified spawn the bot therefore
refused to kill a tower that costs a quarter of the real work and shoots back while we do it. One helper
(`spawnRampartHits`) now answers for all three, and the tick line prints `enemySpawnHits=3000+10000` — the
old line said 3000 and was what made me read "we never reached him" where we were standing on top of him.
The stub learned the same fact: damage to a structure under a rampart goes to the rampart (`hurt()` in
`_world.mjs`), and two scenarios carry it — `fortspawn` (a rampart on the enemy spawn at tick 150, as in
the matches) and `tower+fortspawn`, which is the shape of the draws. All twenty-two older scenarios are
tick-for-tick unchanged.

**And the half that was measured and withdrawn: melee in the wave.** Against a structure `ATTACK` does 30
damage for 80 energy where `RANGED_ATTACK` does 10 for 150 — five times more per energy, and 13000 hits is
where that ratio decides a match. The siege simulation already models everything that argues the other way
(the approach under tower fire, the kiting, the defenders), so it was given both candidate bodies and asked
which one ends the siege sooner; it answered MELEE on the fortified fixture, by a wide margin
(`ranged=lose/25t melee=win/81t`). The stub said no: `tower+fortspawn` went from 1215 to the tick limit,
and the log shows why — twenty-four melee bodies bought, twenty-five fighters alive at the end, and
`push=false(defend)` to the last tick. **The buying rule and the leaving rule disagree**: the siege
simulation values a melee body at its full damage against a structure it must break, while `ourPowerOf`
values it at the kiting discount (`meleeFactor`), so the army that was bought for the assault is never
judged strong enough to leave. Two smaller things were found on the way and are worth keeping in view: the
first version of the comparison preferred melee whenever *both* outcomes were losses ("holding out ten
ticks longer"), which is a taste rule and cost the fixture its win outright; and melee is on a leash in the
target selection (`melee -> target = mySpawn`) that keeps it home even when it is in the wave — the leash
is about chasing a creep across the map, not about a spawn that cannot run, but lifting it alone does not
help while the push gate discounts melee. The removal is named, not written down as a limitation: the
composition and the push decision have to be judged by **one** valuation, and that is a bigger change than
one version — it is the same "two rules disagreeing" defect as v33 and v43, and it is where the next
attempt starts.

**Forty-sixth version — melee joins the wave against an armoured spawn.** v45 recorded that the melee
composition had been measured down and blamed it on two rules using different valuations. That was wrong,
and the log said so: the posture line showed `PUSH ... front=3/3` with twenty-five fighters alive. The
real cause is one line of the wave's membership — `val strikers = fighters.filter { fullSpeed(it) &&
hasRanged(it) }`. A melee body can never be a striker, so it can never be staged, so it can **never** join
an assault; the twenty-four melee bodies bought in that experiment were structurally excluded from every
wave and sat at home as garrison. Three conditions were then tried for admitting melee, and the stub threw
out the first two. (1) *The structural job is larger than the creep job*, measured on the enemies alive at
that instant — this broke every scenario where the enemy comes as a **stream**: in a stream few enemies are
alive at once, so the condition fired and the melee walked into being kited (`stream17` 888 → 1455,
`tower+stream` 947 → 1290, `harass` 779 → 1151). (2) *The siege simulation decides* — it is the right
instrument (it models the approach under tower fire, the defenders and the kiting) and it answered
correctly for the question it was asked, which is the wrong question: with one creep against an unramparted
spawn it says `melee=win/22t` against `ranged=win/65t`, because 3000 hits at 150 a tick really is faster
than at 40. But the body is not bought only for the siege — the same energy is the fleet's guard while the
wave gathers, and `stream17` turned from a win at 888 into **our own spawn destroyed at 1357**. (3) What
holds: the simulation decides, and it is only **asked** when the target is armoured — a rampart on the
spawn or a tower covering it. That is the state the whole thread came from: armour is what makes the job
structural, and 30 damage for 80 energy against 10 for 150 is what makes melee the answer to armour. The
verdict feeds both decisions from one place — who is admitted to the wave and what the spawn buys — so they
cannot disagree. The leash on melee in target selection (`melee -> mySpawn`) is lifted for a creep that is
in the wave: it exists against chasing a kiting creep across the map, and a spawn does not run.

Measured on the whole harness: **every scenario without armour is tick-for-tick identical to v45**, and the
armoured ones move a long way — `tower+healball` 1030 → 521, `tower+stream` 947 → 574, `tower+fortspawn`
1215 → 888, `tower+pairs` 810 → 538, `fortspawn` 508 → 415, `tower` 496 → 450, `towersite` 487 → 437. One
regression stands: `fortress` (three fed towers, no rampart) 1215 → 1332, still a win. Its cause is not
established — the simulation was also taught that a tower fires harder at a melee standing at one than at a
ranged creep standing at three (`towerRangeFor` with the melee's standoff), which is true to the engine and
changed nothing measurable there, so the optimism has another source and it is the next thing to read.

What is still open, and named rather than written down as a limitation: **the siege simulation optimises
the siege in isolation.** It has no way to say "this body is worth more at home for the next three hundred
ticks than at his spawn in eighty", which is exactly what the stream scenarios punish. The gate on armour
is a state condition standing in for that comparison; the comparison itself — one valuation over both jobs —
is the real answer and a bigger piece of work than one version.

**Forty-seventh version — the siege simulation discounts our melee against defenders too.** The
simulation took our damage to the defender queue from `units.sumOf { it.dps() }`, that is 150 a tick for
a melee body, while their damage to us went through `effectiveDps`, that is with the kite discount. One
rule applied to one side. Split in two: `dps()` against the STRUCTURE, which does not run, and
`creepDps()` against the defenders, scaled by the same `meleeFactor` used everywhere else; a body that is
not bought yet has no position, so `meleeReach` asks the question from the parts alone. Every scenario in
the harness is tick-for-tick what v46 gave and the verdicts move only where defenders stand
(`tower+healball` melee 25t → 40t). It closes an asymmetry, not a match — and it is **not** the cause of
the `fortress` optimism it was chased for: there the siege is thirteen ticks and the defender queue is
empty, so what the verdict compares is one tick out of fourteen. That comparison is correct and
irrelevant, and the body it buys is worse at everything else for the next three hundred ticks.

### Twenty matches of v46, and the reading they overturned (07.09.2026)

v46's first series was 5-1-0 and read like a large step. Twenty more matches made it 16-9-1 and −31
rating against v44's 12 at 5-0-5 and +8, and cut per opponent the difference sat entirely on けろびー:
3-0-2 (+11) for v44 against 4-6-1 (−24) for v46. **That reading was wrong, and the operator named why:
opponents version their bots exactly as we version ours.**

`/api/game/<id>` carries `codes[]` — `{user, version}` per side — so every match records which BOT played,
not merely which player. Pooled by name, one けろびー is five different bots in a single afternoon (v13,
v14, v15, v16, v17), and the comparison above added them together. Held apart (`series.py compare`, whose
rows are now one bot each), what v44 and v46 actually share is four bots and nine matches each:

| his bot | our v44 | our v46 |
|---|---|---|
| けろびー v14 | 1-0-2 +1 | 1-0-0 +4 |
| けろびー v15 | 1-0-0 +7 | 1-2-0 −13 |
| stachu3478 v10 | 1-0-2 −2 | 2-1-0 ±0 |
| stachu3478 v7 | 0-0-1 −3 | 2-0-0 +8 |

On the bots both met, v44 is 3-0-5 (+3) and v46 6-3-0 (−1): the draws did turn into decisive matches, and
the rating difference between the versions is not established either way on nine matches. **What moved the
rating was the draw of the matchmaker.** v44 never met けろびー v16, v17 or stachu3478 v6; v46 met them
four times.

The instrument was rebuilt around this and it produced the finding the pooled view had been hiding.
`series.py foes` gives one row per opponent bot with our versions pooled — deliberately pooled, because
"which of their bots beats us" is a question no single version of ours has the matches to ask:

```
opponent bot           n    W-L-D  rating   our versions
けろびー#16                5    0-5-0     -15   v39,v42,v46
けろびー#17                7    0-4-3      -2   v35,v37,v39,v42,v46
stachu3478#6           7    3-4-0     -32   v36,v37,v38,v43,v44,v46
けろびー#15               18   11-4-3     +54   v35,…,v46
```

(the numbers above are the API store's, read 07.09.2026 after the client cache was closed as a
source: the store reaches back two hundred rating matches, the cache had held only what the client
window happened to show. Counting both, けろびー#16 has beaten eight versions of ours in eight
meetings.)

**けろびー#16 has beaten every version of ours it has met; his #17 has never lost to us in seven.** They are not a bad draw, they are a hole — and they are on this disk, with logs
and replays, which is where the next version comes from. Against his v15, by contrast, we are 14-4-3 and
+88: the ladder is not one opponent getting better, it is a set of bots of very different difficulty, and
a series that says nothing about which ones it drew says nothing at all.

Two things stand recorded from the wrong reading. Its arithmetic ("a draw costs nothing, a loss costs
nine, so converting draws into decisive matches is bad") only holds against a bot we cannot beat; against
one we can, the same conversion is what the rating is made of. And the opening deadlock it turned up is
real on its own evidence: `fighterFirst` blocks haulers while the spawn saves for a full body, the saving
is licensed by `holdReady < investReady`, and `investReady` (economy first, then a fighter) grows exactly
when the income is crushed — so the worse the income, the more attractive saving looks. Over the series
that line prints 85 times across eight losses against 20 across nine wins; in one 700-tick loss it prints
28 times, which is 280 ticks of a spawn building nothing while the enemy walks in. The guard put there for
match 17 does not catch it, because its middle clause is true exactly in the state it was meant to break.

### けろびー#16 builds spawns, and that is the whole of it (07.09.2026)

`series.py foes` said けろびー#16 has beaten six versions of ours eight times out of eight and his #17
has never lost to us in seven. Two replays say why, and it is not tactics.

```
tools/replay.py spawns 6a9eb70c…   # his creeps born at t=322 AND t=323 — one tick apart
```

A spawn can start one body at a time, so two births in consecutive ticks mean two spawns. The replay's
object list confirms it: **his #16 has three spawns** — (94,49) from tick 1, (56,14) finished at t=242,
(56,13) at t=544 — and **his #17 has seven**, at (74,21), (51,59), (24,39), (58,54), (29,35) added
through the match, two of them on our half. He pays 1000 for each, out of one 400-energy `M2C2W2`
builder, and the console log had been printing the sites all along: `sites: null(61,31)530/1000` —
`progressTotal` 1000 is `StructureSpawn`, the only structure that costs it.

Our bot took `getObjectsByPrototype(StructureSpawn).firstOrNull { it.my == false }`. One spawn, in his
corner, ninety cells away. So the wave marched past the one he had just built forty cells from us and
ground at the corner while the near one pumped an army into our back — and `pushWinsRace`, which trades
our spawn for his, was offering a trade that cannot be won when he has three.

**What the extra spawns actually buy him is not throughput, it is haulage.** `replay.py economy` on the
match we lost:

```
OURS   picked up  7100 from the piles, delivered  6695 (9.6/tick), spent  8280 on 13 creeps
ENEMY  picked up 16050 from the piles, delivered 15128 (10.6/tick), spent 12440 on 24 creeps
       piles nobody was standing next to when they emptied: 39750 (they decay)
```

The map hands out eighty energy a tick and between us we took a third of it. He collects **2.3 times**
what we do, and his second spawn at (56,14) sits among the containers at (54,13), (57,13) and (56,11):
he moved the delivery point to the energy instead of carrying the energy to the delivery point.

v48 makes the list the state: `enemySpawns` is every live one, `enemySpawn` is the target — the nearest
to ours, both the shortest march and the most dangerous — while map geometry (the distance fields, whose
half a cell is, whose a pile is) stays on his ORIGINAL spawn, because those are facts about the map and
must not travel with the target. `pushWinsRace` refuses the trade outright while he has more than one.
Every scenario in the harness is tick-for-tick what v47 gave; on the new `twospawn` it kills the mid-map
spawn by t=500 where v47 left it untouched until t=1350, and still finishes later overall (1945 against
1443) because a wave with a reachable target goes out, is ground down and comes back — the verdict
volatility, which this exposes rather than causes.

**What v48 does not fix, and the measurement that says so.** Played against the recorded match
(`scenarios/kerobi16.json`), v48 sees all three spawns and targets the near one — and never pushes once
in 743 ticks, because `sim` says lose the whole way: his army is twice ours throughout. The economy is
the binding constraint, and one attempt at it was measured and withdrawn. v49 replaced the fleet-growth
guard — `realised >= projectedIncome * 0.9`, a measurement judged against a model that underrates a long
trip — with the marginal question the guard was really after: did the LAST hauler bought raise delivery.
That is the right question and it changed the wrong thing: the fleet grew from five haulers to nine and
delivery stayed at 10/tick, while `fortress` improved (1332 → 1193), `tower+enemy` lost 68 ticks and the
`siege6` fixture failed outright (240 energy abandoned in a dead site against a 100 threshold). Reverted.

Its value is the number it produced: with nearly twice the fleet our delivery did not move, and
`projectedIncome` fell to **0 of a target of 27** while the map kept dropping two 2000-piles every fifty
ticks. The bot had run out of piles it was *willing* to use: `usable=0` with `sites=2` for the whole
second half. `usableSites` keeps a pile only while `safe` — no armed enemy within `SITE_DANGER_RANGE`
and no damage on the cell — and けろびー's creeps roam and camp, so on his maps everything becomes unsafe
and the income goes to zero. He took 16050 from the same map under the same creeps. **That absolute
safety veto, not the size of the fleet, is what the next version has to answer**, and it should answer
it with the arithmetic the bot already does elsewhere: a pile is worth a trip when the load beats the
chance of losing the hauler, which is the arrival race it already computes for the spawn.

### The delivery point, not the hauler: what the map measures (07.09.2026)

Two levers were proposed against the economy けろびー#16 wins with, and the map settled both before a
line was written. `tools/replay.py scenario` gives the real terrain and the real pile schedule; walking
it with the engine's own fatigue arithmetic (`weight*rate - 2*moves`, then `-2*moves` a tick) answers
directly.

**The hauler body is already right, and the idea of adding MOVE is dead.** On the map of the match we
lost, a fleet hauler's mean round trip is 209 ticks at 1:1 CARRY:MOVE, 190 at 1:2, 180 at 1:3 — a 14%
gain, not the halving the swamp period suggests, because the path already routes *around* swamp: a
loaded 1:1 hauler pays 5 ticks a swamp cell, so Dijkstra simply does not go through it. Per energy
spent, 1:1 delivers 2.39 per 1000 against 1:2's 1.75. Nothing to do here.

**The delivery point is the whole thing.** A temporary pile holds 2000 and decays in 99 ticks, so a pile
further away than its own life is not energy at all — it is scenery. Measured from three points on that
map:

| delivery point | piles reachable before decay | within half their life | mean one-way | energy in reach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| our spawn (5,50) | 18 of 28 | 4 | 82 | 36000 |
| his spawn (94,49) | 22 of 28 | 7 | 75 | 44000 |
| **the spawn he built (56,14)** | **28 of 28** | **17** | **45** | **56000** |

That is the 16050 against our 7100, and the 39750 that decayed with nobody beside it. His 1000 does not
buy production, it buys a shorter trip.

**v49 lands the machinery and not the building, because the building measured as a disaster.** `mySpawns`
is every live spawn of ours, haulers deliver to the nearest with room, the queue runs from whichever free
spawn holds the most energy; with one spawn every branch answers "home" and all twenty-six scenarios are
tick-for-tick what v48 gave.

The other half was written, measured and withdrawn, and the reason is worth keeping. Placing the site is
sound and needs no invented number: candidates are the cells beside energy the map has actually dropped
(`dropHistory`), each scored by the collection rate a fleet of the current capacity would get from it —
capacity over the weighted mean trip, times the share of dropped energy reachable before it decays — and
the site is placed when that gain over the remaining ticks beats the 1000 it costs. On the recorded match
it chose (26,36) and read `rate=3.7->6.4/t`, very nearly doubling. **Then it never got built**, and the
harness went from twenty-six wins to five scenarios ending with OUR spawn destroyed and `none` at 1520
against 428. Three reasons, all in the machinery around sites rather than in the idea:

- the keeper ferries from home, and a forward site is forty cells away — two CARRY over an eighty-tick
  round trip cannot deliver 1000. Teaching it to scoop from the pile it stands beside (v49d) was right
  and not enough;
- `siteInTime`, the clock that decides whether a site can be finished at all, is computed from the
  nearest site to the home spawn and against the home spawn's life under fire. A forward economic site
  is a different question from a home tower and gets the tower's answer;
- and everything downstream — the keeper purchase, the tower's own "is a site already standing", the
  builder's target — keys on `ctx.mySites` as if it held one kind of thing.

So the forward spawn needs the site machinery split by purpose first: which site, whose clock, fed from
where. That is the next piece of work, and it is a refactor before it is a feature.

### v50 — a site is a job, and what that exposed (07.09.2026)

While there was one construction site and it was always the home tower, three different questions
shared one answer. `siteInTime` asked "can a tower be finished before the spawn dies", and the keeper
purchase and the builder's choice of target both read it; "is a site already standing" was
`ctx.mySites.isNotEmpty()`; and the keeper always drew from the spawn. A site of a second kind got the
tower's answer to all three.

A site is now a `SiteJob` carrying its own three:

- **`kind`** — from the price, because a site's cost is its name (tower 1250, spawn 1000). Ramparts and
  extensions share a price and are deliberately absent: the bot does not place them.
- **`deadline`** — what that kind must beat. The spawn's life under fire for a tower, since a tower
  finished after we are razed is not defence; the rest of the match for a delivery point, since it is
  economy and pays back over the match rather than in this fight.
- **`supply`** — where its keeper draws energy, decided by distance and not by kind: the spawn when the
  spawn is nearer, the nearest pile otherwise. One rule that hands the home tower its old answer and a
  forward site the only workable one. A site fed from a pile no longer waits on income reaching the
  spawn, which was another of the tower's answers wrongly applied.

Pure restructuring: twenty-six scenarios tick-for-tick.

**And it exposed a real defect one layer down.** `builderWork` sizes the keeper for the cheapest total
time and that optimum is not bounded by the wallet: at a high enough flow it asks for ten WORK, a body
costing 1200, and the spawn holds a thousand. The branch that saves for a keeper returns every tick
while it cannot afford one, so the spawn builds **nothing** — 176 consecutive "saving for builder
cost=1200 energy=1000" lines and two fighters in a whole match. The optimum is now cut by what can be
bought. At the flows a match reaches it asks for five WORK and nothing moves, but the flow it reads is
a projection and a projection spikes.

**The forward spawn was rebuilt on this machinery and still measured as a loss — for a new reason, and
a sharper one.** Placement and payback both work: it chose (65,39) reading `rate=4.8->6.1/t`, and with
the tower no longer confused by a foreign site every scenario stayed a win. But every scenario was 30
to 500 ticks slower (`tower+hover` 450 → 984, `tower+stream` 574 → 1014) and the site never went past
`40/1000`. The reason is arithmetic and was not visible before: **one keeper with two CARRY cannot move
a thousand energy.** It can only put in what it carries — 100 a trip — and the pile the site was placed
beside has usually decayed by the time it gets there, so the trip is not two cells but forty. けろびー
solves it by building where a pile is alive at that moment, at two WORK next to his own supply.

**The third attempt, on the same day, fixed both of those and still measured as a loss — and named the
reason the first two hid.** The placement was wrong for a reason worth keeping: candidates were only
cells beside past drops, and the best cell on the map is the middle, which no drop had touched. Scanning
the neighbourhood of the energy-weighted centroid as well finds it: on the map of the match we lost, the
fleet collects **20.0 energy a tick delivering home and 46.1 delivering to (49,46)**. The keeper was
wrong for a reason that is pure arithmetic: `[MOVE×2, CARRY×2, WORK×k]` carries a hundred, and a spawn
costs a thousand. Sizing it for the job — enough CARRY that ferrying keeps up with the hands, which is
`c = w·d/5` from `d·C/(25c) = C/(5w)`, MOVE for the weight, the whole thing cut by what the spawn can
hold — took the site from 40/1000 to 600–800/1000.

And every scenario was still slower (`none` 428 → 460, `ball` 663 → 872, `tower+healball` 521 → 1028),
with the spawn never finished. Adding the honest horizon — the match ends when our siege ends, so the
investment's working life is `min(ticksLimit, travel + siege.ticks)` rather than the arena's limit —
changed almost nothing, because the decision is taken around tick 100, when nobody's siege is winnable
and the horizon is therefore "the whole match" in every scenario.

**The harness cannot judge this feature, and that is the finding.** Every scenario in it is a short win
— 430 to 900 ticks — so a thousand energy taken out of the army at tick 100 is always worse there than
the income it returns by tick 460. The matches the feature exists for are the ones we lose: けろびー#16
at 743 and #17 at 2000, where our siege is never winnable and the game is decided by who collects more.
The stub's opponents cannot produce that, by construction.

What the arithmetic still misses is the same thing in a sentence: **the price is paid out of the army at
the moment the army decides the match**, and `forwardWorth` compares the investment only against doing
nothing, never against the fighter the same thousand would buy. `towerWorth` already does exactly that
comparison for the tower — gain in defence per energy, against a fighter, discounted by measured
mortality — and the delivery point needs its own version of it. That, and a scenario in the harness
where the enemy is strong enough that the match is long, are what the fourth attempt needs.

**Re-measured on the long scenarios, which is what should have been done first.** The third attempt was
judged on twenty-four scenarios that end in a win at 430–900 ticks, and an investment that pays 26 energy
a tick from tick ~250 cannot win there — by construction, not by defect. On `twospawn`, the one long
scenario in the harness, the same build takes the match from **1945 to 1373 ticks, a 29% gain**, and on
none of the short scenarios did it turn a win into a loss: they were slower, and slower wins cost nothing.
That is the measurement the feature deserved, and reading the tick count of a short scenario as a verdict
was my error, not the harness's.

Two things still stop it landing, and both are sharp now.

`siege6` — the fixture that says a spawn must not pour energy into a site it cannot finish — fails at 720
of 1000. Bounding the forward site's deadline by the home spawn's life (feasibility: the keeper is born
and fed from home, so the site must outlive it) brought that down from 880 but not away: the site is
placed at tick ~100 and the siege arrives later, so the energy is already sunk when the deadline turns.

And the obvious gate — ask for a delivery point only when the enemy has more than one spawn, the state
that makes a match economic by construction and the one けろびー#16 and #17 are in — makes it **worse than
not having it at all**: the second spawn of his appears around tick 240, so ours is placed at ~674, reaches
70 of 1000, and `twospawn` goes from a win at 1945 to no win at all in 2000. Late investment is pure loss.

So the shape of the problem is now exact: **the decision has to be taken early, and early there is nothing
that tells a long match from a short one.** Every signal the bot has — the siege verdict, his spawn count,
his army — says the same thing at tick 100 in every match. What is left is to make the bet cheap enough to
be worth taking blind (a smaller first structure, or a site that can be abandoned without loss) or to find
a signal in the opening that separates the regimes. The site machinery (v50) is not what blocks it, and
the three attempts are recorded so the fourth does not rediscover them.

### Twenty matches of v50 — the best rating rate measured so far (07.09.2026)

Four versions had landed without a single live match (v47's siege symmetry, v48's plural enemy spawns,
v49's plural own spawns, v50's site jobs and the keeper-body fix). Twenty matches:

| version | n | W-L-D | rating | per match |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| v44 | 20 | 14-1-5 | +9 | +0.5 |
| v46 | 26 | 16-9-1 | −31 | −1.2 |
| **v50** | **20** | **16-3-1** | **+28** | **+1.4** |

Rating 1189 → 1217. Cut per opponent BOT against v46, on the bots both versions met:

| his bot | v46 | v50 |
|---|---|---|
| stachu3478#7 | 2-0-0 +8 | 4-0-0 +20 |
| stachu3478#9 | 0-1-0 −10 | 2-0-0 +11 |
| stachu3478#6 | 0-1-0 −11 | 1-0-0 +5 |
| stachu3478#10 | 2-1-0 ±0 | 1-0-0 +5 |
| けろびー#15 | 1-2-0 −13 | 2-1-0 +5 |
| けろびー#13 | 2-1-1 −7 | 0-1-0 −11 |

Thirteen matches each on shared bots: v46 10-6-2, v50 13-3-1. stachu3478#6 and #9, which had leaked
−46 and −10 over their history, both went positive.

Two things this series does **not** say. It never drew けろびー#16 or #17 — the two bots no version of
ours has beaten — so the standing hole is untested; and it cannot attribute the gain to any one of the
four versions, since they were played together. The three losses were けろびー#13, けろびー#15 and a
first meeting with `Little Ancestor#1` (−15); both けろびー losses had `enemySpawns=1`, so v48's change
took no part in them.

### v51 — the healer, and the two defects it exposed (07.09.2026)

**Where the rating actually leaks.** Over the forty matches of 07.09 the cut per opponent BOT is not
even: けろびー's six bots are 6-8-2 for **−41**, and the other thirteen bots together are 21-3-0 for
**+32**. Every point lost is lost to him.

**What five of his replays say.** His healers ran at 100% uptime in all five, ours do not exist:

| match | our damage | he healed | our NET on him | his damage | we healed |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| L けろびー#13 | 14780 | 1920 | 12860 | 10260 | **0** |
| L けろびー#15 | 7850 | 4308 | 3542 | 9290 | **0** |
| W けろびー#15 | 15990 | **10044** | 5946 | 8370 | **0** |
| W けろびー#15 | 14210 | 5628 | 8582 | 5710 | **0** |
| D けろびー#14 | 32480 | 2276 | 30204 | 8900 | **0** |

In the 1900-tick win we fired 15990 and he healed 10044 of it away; the win came on a net of 5946 at
twice his spend on damage. His queue in the log names the body: `M1C1` haulers, `M3R3`/`M5R5` shooters
and **`M5H3` — a pure healer, 1000 energy, 36 healing a tick at range 1** — from tick 220 on.

Two other numbers from the same replays, both recorded and neither acted on yet: our melee is adjacent
to his creeps for 5, 12, 12 and 66 creep-ticks out of 801–4508 (**0.3–1.5%**; he carries no ATTACK and
kites at 7), and our shooters have something within 3 for **3–13%** of their armed ticks.

**The instrument could not see a healer at all.** What decides which body to buy is the siege
simulation, and it was asymmetric: it subtracted the DEFENDERS' healing from our damage
(`net = creepDps − defs.heal`) and knew nothing of ours. A body with no damage, in a model with no
healing, cannot do anything — so "what if a healer" answered "strictly worse" **by construction**, not
by measurement. The 05.09 note that rejected HEAL parts *in a fighter's body* had already named the
next candidate correctly: a separate healer creep beside a full shooter, a formation of two rather than
one body. So: our units mend in the run, `healerBody` is the third candidate beside ranged and melee,
and a creep that heals counts as being in arms — in the wave, in the defenders, and dropped from the
wave when its HEAL is shot off, exactly like a shooter whose guns are gone.

**Two defects the healer exposed, both older than it.**

*The body question was asked about the wrong group.* The run answers "what if ONE more body like this",
and the answer then governs **every** purchase until it changes. For it to change, what we buy has to
enter the group the question is asked about — and the group was `waveFront`, the four creeps inside the
cohesion gap, which reinforcements never join. With melee this was invisible: melee still shoots, and
an army of melee still breaks a spawn. With a healer it was not: `tower+enemy` bought 31 healers and
four shooters and stopped winning at all where it had won at 827. The question is now asked about the
whole marching army (`offensive`, full-speed only).

*The march could hang the bot.* The approach paced itself by `wave.maxOf { periodAt(...) }`, and a
creep with no live MOVE has period `Int.MAX_VALUE / 4` — `repeat(536870911)`. As soon as the whole army
entered the question, so did a creep shot off its legs, and `tower+healball` stopped dead at tick 480.
The march is now paced by whoever can still walk, and a wave that cannot walk loses the siege. This one
would have hung a live match.

**The run credits the healer at `RANGED_HEAL_POWER`, not `HEAL_POWER`.** Adjacent healing is three
times stronger and 96% of his heals were adjacent (80+3r, 120+0r, 279+5r, 155+7r, 95+4r) — but adjacent
is a POSITION, and this simulation has none: it treats the wave as a point. That assumption is
conservative for damage (everyone is in the blast) and generous for healing, and at the full rate the
run promised one healer would end the siege eleven ticks before a fifth shooter (`tower+enemy`: 56
against 67). Acted on, that promise sent the wave out five-strong instead of six, it died, the army
dribbled out in seven waves after it, and the match was not won at all. What the run credits now is
what a healer gives WITHOUT a position; the rest is paid for by formation, and formation is not
something the run promises.

**Measured.** All 26 stub scenarios and four recorded opponents: nothing worse, `tower+fortspawn`
888 → 868 and the recorded `kerobi-win` 1523 → 1478. A healer is chosen in one scenario (`fortress`).

**Where it does not reach, and why that is now known rather than guessed.** けろびー beats us at HOME,
and this question is only asked on the assault path. But the recorded `kerobi16` says the home loss is
not a healing problem at all: the bot buys six full M8R4 and then sits in the `fighter first` deadlock
(`closes=false`, deficit 708–831) with 847 energy in the spawn, at `income=9/27` against his 24 creeps
to our 13. That is an ECONOMY loss with a spawn-queue deadlock on top, and adding a defensive healer to
it would have been machinery built on a guess.

### Twenty matches of v51 — rank 1, and the first win over けろびー#16 (07.09.2026)

| version | n | W-L-D | rating | per match |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| v44 | 20 | 14-1-5 | +9 | +0.5 |
| v46 | 20 | 11-8-1 | −37 | −1.9 |
| v50 | 20 | 16-3-1 | +28 | +1.4 |
| **v51** | **20** | **17-3-0** | **+46** | **+2.3** |

Rating 1217 → 1263, **rank 1**. The draw against けろびー is what he draws when we hold: v51 had none.

The series finally met the standing hole. Against his bots: #13 **2-0** (+6, v50 was 0-1), #15 **2-0**
(+11), #14 **1-0** (+3, v50 drew), #11 1-0, #7 1-0, **#16 1-2 (+7)** and #17 0-1 (−3). Eight-three
against けろびー overall, **+25**, where v46 pooled 6-8-2 for −41. **#16 has now been beaten once — the
first time any version of ours has.** #17 is still unbeaten, on one match.

**And the gain is not the healer.** In twenty live matches a healer was chosen on exactly one tick and
**built zero times**; every line of the healer machinery is inert without one. What actually changed
behaviour is the defect the healer exposed — the assault-body question moving from the four-creep front
to the whole marching army — and the verdict counts say how much:

| | melee | ranged | healer |
|---|---|---|---|
| v50 (asked about the front) | 490 | 17 | — |
| v51 (asked about the army) | 435 | 445 | 1 |

Asked about four creeps in the cohesion gap, the run said MELEE on **97%** of the ticks it was asked:
in a group that small, melee's five-times-cheaper damage against a structure dominates everything else.
Asked about the army that will actually march, it splits about evenly — and the bot bought 31 melee
bodies across the series where v50 bought 48. That single correction is worth +18 rating over twenty
matches against a comparable draw, and it is what beat #16.

So the honest ledger of v51: the healer is machinery that has not yet paid for itself, the two defects
it exposed are what paid, and one of them (the approach paced by a creep with no live MOVE) would have
hung a live match outright.

### The "fighter first" deadlock, proposed and rejected by its own measurement (07.09.2026)

The v51 series offered a clean-looking separator: `spawn.first` is **1.0 in all three losses** and
0.0–0.2 in fifteen of seventeen wins. Beside it, in every loss, `spawn.closes=false` held for 31–36
ticks — the affordable body does not close the deficit, so nothing gets built — while the fleet sat at
4–6 haulers against 7–9 in the wins and the spawn held 464 energy against 380. In the recorded
`kerobi16` the same state shows 847 energy in the spawn at `income=9/27` while it is being torn down.
The reading was: two rules that each have a reason (`боец первым` from matches 12/17, `недомерок только
если закрывает дефицит` from match 12) together refuse to spend anything.

The fix followed the shape the frames ask for — computed, not tuned: **hold only if what you are
waiting for changes the outcome.** The rule asked `closesDeficit` about the body it could afford NOW;
it should ask about the FULL body it is holding for, since a full body that does not close the deficit
will be bought and change nothing, while the haulers it forbade are the only thing that grows one that
will. One line: `fighterFirst` gains `&& (fullCloses || closesNow)`.

**It does not survive its own measurement, in two independent ways.**

*The fixture says no.* `camped19` — enemy adjacent, no income, spawn alive about fifty ticks, 911 energy
on hand — went from building a 900-cost fighter to building two haulers and dying with nothing. That
fixture exists for exactly this (match 19: 904 in the bank, zero creeps, destroyed at tick 1000). Under
fire there is no "later" to invest for: income cannot arrive before death, and the last thousand has to
become a body. The rule as written cannot tell that case from the one it was meant for, because the
thing that separates them is not the deficit but whether the spawn is being shot (`fire=false` at every
purchase in all three live losses, `fire=true` in `camped19`).

*The series says the premise is wrong.* Counting how many ticks each match printed `fighter first`:

| | ticks holding | haulers built |
|---|---|---|
| the three losses | 31, 36, 36 | 6, 10, 5 |
| the seventeen wins | 0 in eight of them — and **64**, 29, 12, 7, 6, 3, 2, 2, 1 in the rest | 5–17, median 12 |

A win held sixty-four times and bought thirteen haulers. Holding is not what stops the fleet growing,
and `spawn.first` separates the outcomes without being their cause. The deficits actually held for were
1–419, which is not "waiting for something that cannot help" — it is the rule working.

**What does separate, on the same evidence.** Haulers per hundred ticks: losses 0.63, 0.75, 1.0; wins
1.0–1.7. And the collection race, with the strongest control available — the SAME opponent bot:

| vs けろびー#16 | we collected | he collected | result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1539 ticks | **21800** | 18600 | won |
| 843 ticks | 7150 | **16900** | lost |

One bot, two outcomes, and the ratio flips. The map hands out 80 a tick; we take 9.7–12.4 and 48000 to
91900 decays with nobody beside it. That is the delivery point again, and it is where the next attempt
belongs — not in the spawn queue.

### Extensions: what is established, what is not, and one trap (07.09.2026)

The body ceiling without extensions is **exactly `SPAWN_ENERGY_CAPACITY = 1000`**, because the whole
